It really is just the tedious stuff, like the paperwork, that I can't seem to make myself do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. It's just that mental block when it comes to the boring tasks, not a lack of physical energy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because you're trying to avoid the feelings you get when you think about doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or long insurance papers to read through, or... even just a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's just that my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just avoid doing it altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable or distressed in some way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't use the word distressed, no. I'm not upset or anxious about doing them. It's not like the paperwork scares me or makes me feel bad... it really is just that mental wall I mentioned. I just can't bring myself to focus on all those details, so I go find something else to do. But I don't feel emotionally uncomfortable, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just not interested in doing those tasks?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a matter of being interested or not. I mean, nobody's really interested in paying bills or filling out Medicare forms, right? But other people just sit down and get them done. For me, it's not about not wanting to do it... it's more like my brain just refuses to engage with it. I know it needs to be done, but I just can't make myself focus on it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It really is just the tedious stuff, like the paperwork, that I can't seem to make myself do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. It's just that mental block when it comes to the boring tasks, not a lack of physical energy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because you're trying to avoid the feelings you get when you think about doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or long insurance papers to read through, or... even just a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's just that my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just avoid doing it altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable or distressed in some way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't use the word distressed, no. I'm not upset or anxious about doing them. It's not like the paperwork scares me or makes me feel bad... it really is just that mental wall I mentioned. I just can't bring myself to focus on all those details, so I go find something else to do. But I don't feel emotionally uncomfortable, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just not interested in doing those tasks?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a matter of being interested or not. I mean, nobody's really interested in paying bills or filling out Medicare forms, right? But other people just sit down and get them done. For me, it's not about not wanting to do it... it's more like my brain just refuses to engage with it. I know it needs to be done, but I just can't make myself focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are enjoying things less often than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed... spending time with my grandchildren, gardening, reading a good book. I still look forward to those things and get pleasure out of them just like I always have. It's just the tedious things I struggle with, not the things I actually like doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy the tasks you've been putting off if you were able to get started on them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, it's paying bills and filling out paperwork. I don't think anyone actually enjoys doing those things, whether they have trouble starting them or not. It's just chores that have to get done. So no, I wouldn't say I'd enjoy them if I could just get going on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could focus on these tasks if someone else was doing them with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't actually tried having anyone sit down and do my paperwork with me. I suppose it might help to have someone right there to keep me on track, but... well, I honestly just don't know if it would make a difference for me or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to start these tasks if they were made more enjoyable or rewarding for you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, how do you make paying the electric bill or sorting through Medicare forms enjoyable? It's just paperwork. I suppose if there was some sort of big reward at the end, maybe? But I've never really tried to bribe myself to do it. So I honestly couldn't tell you if that would work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deliberately delaying tasks until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or... insurance papers I need to read through, or even putting together a program schedule, I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just keep pushing it back until I absolutely have to do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF48.8, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I just can't seem to get anything in order anymore. My kitchen table is completely covered with things—my husband's appointment papers, flyers from church, notes from the senior center, and all these half-finished lists. I sit there looking at it, and... I just don't know which thing to do first. I end up sitting there not doing any of it until the whole day is just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things or learning new information?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My memory is actually pretty good. I don't have any trouble learning new things, either. I know exactly what all those papers on my table are for, and I remember what I'm supposed to do with them. I just... I just can't seem to actually get started on any of it. So no, I wouldn't say remembering is the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you want to get started on these tasks, but something is holding you back?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do want to get them done. But... well, when there's a form to fill out, or insurance papers I need to read through, or even just putting together a program schedule for the center, I just... I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head just feels completely worn out before I even start. I look at the paper and it just feels like too much effort to actually sit down and work through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's anxiety. I'm not nervous about doing them, or worried about getting them wrong. And I wouldn't say I panic or anything like that. It's really just... I look at the pile and it just feels like too much work for my brain to handle right then. So I just walk away from it instead of dealing with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. I don't have any trouble with things I actually like doing. If it's something I enjoy, I... well, I have no problem getting started on that at all. I still have plenty of motivation for my hobbies and things like that. It really is just the tedious stuff, like the paperwork, that I can't seem to make myself do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. It's just that mental block when it comes to the boring tasks, not a lack of physical energy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because you're trying to avoid the feelings you get when you think about doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or long insurance papers to read through, or... even just a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's just that my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just avoid doing it altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable or distressed in some way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't use the word distressed, no. I'm not upset or anxious about doing them. It's not like the paperwork scares me or makes me feel bad... it really is just that mental wall I mentioned. I just can't bring myself to focus on all those details, so I go find something else to do. But I don't feel emotionally uncomfortable, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just not interested in doing those tasks?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a matter of being interested or not. I mean, nobody's really interested in paying bills or filling out Medicare forms, right? But other people just sit down and get them done. For me, it's not about not wanting to do it... it's more like my brain just refuses to engage with it. I know it needs to be done, but I just can't make myself focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are enjoying things less often than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed... spending time with my grandchildren, gardening, reading a good book. I still look forward to those things and get pleasure out of them just like I always have. It's just the tedious things I struggle with, not the things I actually like doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy the tasks you've been putting off if you were able to get started on them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, it's paying bills and filling out paperwork. I don't think anyone actually enjoys doing those things, whether they have trouble starting them or not. It's just chores that have to get done. So no, I wouldn't say I'd enjoy them if I could just get going on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could focus on these tasks if someone else was doing them with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't actually tried having anyone sit down and do my paperwork with me. I suppose it might help to have someone right there to keep me on track, but... well, I honestly just don't know if it would make a difference for me or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to start these tasks if they were made more enjoyable or rewarding for you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, how do you make paying the electric bill or sorting through Medicare forms enjoyable? It's just paperwork. I suppose if there was some sort of big reward at the end, maybe? But I've never really tried to bribe myself to do it. So I honestly couldn't tell you if that would work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deliberately delaying tasks until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or... insurance papers I need to read through, or even putting together a program schedule, I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So, no, I wouldn't say I act impulsively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off tasks because you expect them to be boring or unpleasant?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd say that's the reason. I don't really look at a stack of mail or a form and think about how boring it's going to be. It's not really about it being unpleasant, either. My kitchen table is completely covered with things—my husband's appointment papers, flyers from church, notes from the senior center, and all these half-finished lists. I sit there looking at it, and... I just don't know which thing to do first. My memory is actually pretty good. I don't have any trouble learning new things, either. I know exactly what all those papers on my table are for, and I remember what I'm supposed to do with them. I just... I just can't seem to actually get started on any of it. So no, I wouldn't say remembering is the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you want to get started on these tasks, but something is holding you back?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do want to get them done. But... well, when there's a form to fill out, or insurance papers I need to read through, or even just putting together a program schedule for the center, I just... I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head just feels completely worn out before I even start. I look at the paper and it just feels like too much effort to actually sit down and work through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's anxiety. I'm not nervous about doing them, or worried about getting them wrong. And I wouldn't say I panic or anything like that. It's really just... I look at the pile and it just feels like too much work for my brain to handle right then. So I just walk away from it instead of dealing with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. I don't have any trouble with things I actually like doing. If it's something I enjoy, I... well, I have no problem getting started on that at all. I still have plenty of motivation for my hobbies and things like that. It really is just the tedious stuff, like the paperwork, that I can't seem to make myself do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. It's just that mental block when it comes to the boring tasks, not a lack of physical energy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because you're trying to avoid the feelings you get when you think about doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or long insurance papers to read through, or... even just a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's just that my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just avoid doing it altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable or distressed in some way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't use the word distressed, no. I'm not upset or anxious about doing them. It's not like the paperwork scares me or makes me feel bad... it really is just that mental wall I mentioned. I just can't bring myself to focus on all those details, so I go find something else to do. But I don't feel emotionally uncomfortable, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just not interested in doing those tasks?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a matter of being interested or not. I mean, nobody's really interested in paying bills or filling out Medicare forms, right? But other people just sit down and get them done. For me, it's not about not wanting to do it... it's more like my brain just refuses to engage with it. I know it needs to be done, but I just can't make myself focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are enjoying things less often than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed... spending time with my grandchildren, gardening, reading a good book. I still look forward to those things and get pleasure out of them just like I always have. It's just the tedious things I struggle with, not the things I actually like doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy the tasks you've been putting off if you were able to get started on them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, it's paying bills and filling out paperwork. I don't think anyone actually enjoys doing those things, whether they have trouble starting them or not. It's just chores that have to get done. So no, I wouldn't say I'd enjoy them if I could just get going on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could focus on these tasks if someone else was doing them with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't actually tried having anyone sit down and do my paperwork with me. I suppose it might help to have someone right there to keep me on track, but... well, I honestly just don't know if it would make a difference for me or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to start these tasks if they were made more enjoyable or rewarding for you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, how do you make paying the electric bill or sorting through Medicare forms enjoyable? It's just paperwork. I suppose if there was some sort of big reward at the end, maybe? But I've never really tried to bribe myself to do it. So I honestly couldn't tell you if that would work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deliberately delaying tasks until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or... insurance papers I need to read through, or even putting together a program schedule, I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just keep pushing it back until I absolutely have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting impulsively, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't really look at a stack of mail or a form and think about how boring it's going to be. It's not really about it being unpleasant, either. It's just... well, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's more that I feel completely drained before I even begin. I don't really sit around expecting to be bored by it. So, no, I haven't really noticed that being the cause.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to complete these tasks if someone was counting on you to get them done?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. My kitchen table is completely covered with things—my husband's appointment papers, flyers from church, notes from the senior center, and all these half-finished lists. I sit there looking at it, and... I just don't know which thing to do first. My memory is actually pretty good. I don't have any trouble learning new things, either. I know exactly what all those papers on my table are for, and I remember what I'm supposed to do with them. I just... I just can't seem to actually get started on any of it. So no, I wouldn't say remembering is the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you want to get started on these tasks, but something is holding you back?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do want to get them done. But... well, when there's a form to fill out, or insurance papers I need to read through, or even just putting together a program schedule for the center, I just... I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head just feels completely worn out before I even start. I look at the paper and it just feels like too much effort to actually sit down and work through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's anxiety. I'm not nervous about doing them, or worried about getting them wrong. And I wouldn't say I panic or anything like that. It's really just... I look at the pile and it just feels like too much work for my brain to handle right then. So I just walk away from it instead of dealing with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. I don't have any trouble with things I actually like doing. If it's something I enjoy, I... well, I have no problem getting started on that at all. I still have plenty of motivation for my hobbies and things like that. It really is just the tedious stuff, like the paperwork, that I can't seem to make myself do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. It's just that mental block when it comes to the boring tasks, not a lack of physical energy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because you're trying to avoid the feelings you get when you think about doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or long insurance papers to read through, or... even just a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's just that my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just avoid doing it altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable or distressed in some way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't use the word distressed, no. I'm not upset or anxious about doing them. It's not like the paperwork scares me or makes me feel bad... it really is just that mental wall I mentioned. I just can't bring myself to focus on all those details, so I go find something else to do. But I don't feel emotionally uncomfortable, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just not interested in doing those tasks?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a matter of being interested or not. I mean, nobody's really interested in paying bills or filling out Medicare forms, right? But other people just sit down and get them done. For me, it's not about not wanting to do it... it's more like my brain just refuses to engage with it. I know it needs to be done, but I just can't make myself focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are enjoying things less often than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed... spending time with my grandchildren, gardening, reading a good book. I still look forward to those things and get pleasure out of them just like I always have. It's just the tedious things I struggle with, not the things I actually like doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy the tasks you've been putting off if you were able to get started on them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, it's paying bills and filling out paperwork. I don't think anyone actually enjoys doing those things, whether they have trouble starting them or not. It's just chores that have to get done. So no, I wouldn't say I'd enjoy them if I could just get going on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could focus on these tasks if someone else was doing them with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't actually tried having anyone sit down and do my paperwork with me. I suppose it might help to have someone right there to keep me on track, but... well, I honestly just don't know if it would make a difference for me or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to start these tasks if they were made more enjoyable or rewarding for you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, how do you make paying the electric bill or sorting through Medicare forms enjoyable? It's just paperwork. I suppose if there was some sort of big reward at the end, maybe? But I've never really tried to bribe myself to do it. So I honestly couldn't tell you if that would work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deliberately delaying tasks until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or... insurance papers I need to read through, or even putting together a program schedule, I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head feels completely worn out before I even start. If anything, I probably think too much about how exhausting a task is going to be. So, no, I wouldn't say I act impulsively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off tasks because you expect them to be boring or unpleasant?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd say that's the reason. forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve missed returning calls, left bills sitting under a stack of mail, forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: Even when I’m trying to sit politely in a meeting, I catch myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around so much that people look over at me.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining Even if I have the whole week to fill out a form, I still look at it and just feel that overwhelming exhaustion. So, no, having extra time doesn't really seem to help me get it done any sooner.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would be able to make a decision about what task to do first if someone else presented you with only two options?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF48.8, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I just can't seem to get anything in order anymore. My kitchen table is completely covered with things—my husband's appointment papers, flyers from church, notes from the senior center, and all these half-finished lists. I sit there looking at it, and... I just don't know which thing to do first. I end up sitting there not doing any of it until the whole day is just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things or learning new information?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My memory is actually pretty good. I don't have any trouble learning new things, either. I know exactly what all those papers on my table are for, and I remember what I'm supposed to do with them. I just... I just can't seem to actually get started on any of it. So no, I wouldn't say remembering is the issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you want to get started on these tasks, but something is holding you back?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do want to get them done. But... well, when there's a form to fill out, or insurance papers I need to read through, or even just putting together a program schedule for the center, I just... I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head just feels completely worn out before I even start. I look at the paper and it just feels like too much effort to actually sit down and work through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you think about tackling these tasks?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's anxiety. I'm not nervous about doing them, or worried about getting them wrong. And I wouldn't say I panic or anything like that. It's really just... I look at the pile and it just feels like too much work for my brain to handle right then. So I just walk away from it instead of dealing with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel motivated to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. I don't have any trouble with things I actually like doing. If it's something I enjoy, I... well, I have no problem getting started on that at all. I still have plenty of motivation for my hobbies and things like that. It really is just the tedious stuff, like the paperwork, that I can't seem to make myself do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I don't feel sad or down. I mean, I get a bit frustrated with myself sometimes because I can't just sit and do the paperwork like I should, but I'm not depressed. My mood is generally pretty good.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or lacking in energy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually tired. I mean, I'm 70, so I don't have the energy I had when I was 20, but I have enough energy to get through my day. I don't feel wiped out or exhausted all the time. It's just that mental block when it comes to the boring tasks, not a lack of physical energy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be putting off these tasks because you're trying to avoid the feelings you get when you think about doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or long insurance papers to read through, or... even just a program schedule to put together, I keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's just that my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just avoid doing it altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're avoiding these tasks because they make you feel uncomfortable or distressed in some way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't use the word distressed, no. I'm not upset or anxious about doing them. It's not like the paperwork scares me or makes me feel bad... it really is just that mental wall I mentioned. I just can't bring myself to focus on all those details, so I go find something else to do. But I don't feel emotionally uncomfortable, if that's what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just not interested in doing those tasks?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really a matter of being interested or not. I mean, nobody's really interested in paying bills or filling out Medicare forms, right? But other people just sit down and get them done. For me, it's not about not wanting to do it... it's more like my brain just refuses to engage with it. I know it needs to be done, but I just can't make myself focus on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you are enjoying things less often than you used to?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed... spending time with my grandchildren, gardening, reading a good book. I still look forward to those things and get pleasure out of them just like I always have. It's just the tedious things I struggle with, not the things I actually like doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would enjoy the tasks you've been putting off if you were able to get started on them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Like I said, it's paying bills and filling out paperwork. I don't think anyone actually enjoys doing those things, whether they have trouble starting them or not. It's just chores that have to get done. So no, I wouldn't say I'd enjoy them if I could just get going on them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could focus on these tasks if someone else was doing them with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't actually tried having anyone sit down and do my paperwork with me. I suppose it might help to have someone right there to keep me on track, but... well, I honestly just don't know if it would make a difference for me or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to start these tasks if they were made more enjoyable or rewarding for you?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, how do you make paying the electric bill or sorting through Medicare forms enjoyable? It's just paperwork. I suppose if there was some sort of big reward at the end, maybe? But I've never really tried to bribe myself to do it. So I honestly couldn't tell you if that would work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself deliberately delaying tasks until the last minute?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Whenever there’s a form to fill out, or... insurance papers I need to read through, or even putting together a program schedule, I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. It's like my head feels completely worn out before I even start. So I just keep pushing it back until I absolutely have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting impulsively, doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I have a problem with that. I mean, I might put things off, but I don't just jump into things without thinking. If anything, I probably think too much about how exhausting a task is going to be. So, no, I wouldn't say I act impulsively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself putting off tasks because you expect them to be boring or unpleasant?\n\nPatient: I'm not sure I'd say that's the reason. I don't really look at a stack of mail or a form and think about how boring it's going to be. It's not really about it being unpleasant, either. It's just... well, like I mentioned a minute ago, it's more that I feel completely drained before I even begin. I don't really sit around expecting to be bored by it. So, no, I haven't really noticed that being the cause.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to complete these tasks if someone was counting on you to get them done?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I haven't noticed if that makes a difference or not. I mean... even if someone is waiting on me to finish a piece of paperwork, I still get that same exhausted feeling before I even start. So, I don't know if having someone count on me would actually make it any easier to just sit down and get it done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still put off these tasks even if you knew you had enough time to complete them without feeling rushed?\n\nPatient: I don't think having more time would really change anything. I mean, I'm retired, so I usually have plenty of time. It's not a matter of feeling rushed. Even if I have the whole week to fill out a form, I still look at it and just feel that overwhelming exhaustion. So, no, having extra time doesn't really seem to help me get it done any sooner.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to make a decision about what task to do first if someone else presented you with only two options?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't been in a situation recently where someone just gave me two things to pick from like that. Usually, it's just me looking at the things I need to do around the house or the mail I need to sort. I don't know if having someone narrow it down to two choices would make it easier to start or not. I just haven't noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
